Title: Blog about the case report: "TAV-in-TAV-in-TAV after treated endocarditis: procedural strategy, imaging planning and 1-year outcome"

# Beyond the Fourth Time? Feasibility of 'Triple-Stacked' TAVR After Endocarditis

We've all heard the mantra: "Once a TAVR fails, it's usually the last one before surgery." Then comes the era of *Valve-in-Valve*, and we accept that a second life is possible if the annulus holds up. But push past two layers, add a history of **prosthetic valve endocarditis (PVE)**, and suddenly you're staring down the barrel of something most guidelines would politely suggest is impossible. Yet, here we are with a fascinating case report from Merkulov et al., detailing a **"TAV-in-TAV-in-TAV"** procedure successfully performed in a septuagenarian male after treated endocarditis.

This isn't just about stacking metal rings; it's about rewriting the rulebook on lifetime management for structural heart disease. The patient, now in his late 70s, presents with a cardiovascular résumé that reads like a thriller novel: three separate TAVRs over a decade, a bout of *Enterococcus* endocarditis requiring six weeks of IV antibiotics, and eventual catastrophic hemodynamic collapse from severe restenosis. Traditional thinking screams "Surgical Explant," but given his EuroSCORE II of nearly 28%, COPD, diabetic nephropathy-like physiology, and three-vessel CAD status, open surgery was effectively contraindicated. The team didn't back down; they leaned heavily on modern CT physics and strategic valve selection to pull off what was thought to be non-feasible.

Let's break down the chronology because the sequence dictates the solution. Back in 2014, our protagonist got his first ride: a **Lotus** valve (the old Boston Scientific adjustable-flow marvel). It held until 2021 when things went south fast—gradients spiked to 83 mmHg, and LV function tanked to 42%. Enter stage right: **TAV-in-TAV #1**. They deployed an Evolut R/Medtronic self-expanding valve inside the Lotus in 2022. Hemodynamics improved initially (EF bounced to 54%), but fate intervened shortly thereafter with *E. faecalis* bacteremia and frank PVE. After aggressive antimicrobial stewardship confirmed cure via negative cultures and TOE, the valve began failing again due to mixed mechanisms: likely leaflet fibrosis from infection combined with standard structural decay.

By mid-2024, gradients were climbing again (49 mmHg mean), AVA collapsed to 0.5 cm², and regurgitation returned. The question wasn't whether to intervene—it was how? You cannot simply throw any valve into this mosh pit of stent struts without risking immediate coronary occlusion or creating an impenetrable neo-laminar barrier leading to severe Prosthesis-Patient Mismatch (PPM).

The secret sauce here was **meticulous CT planning** coupled with specific device architecture choices. The team utilized multislice CT (MSCT) not just for sizing, but for virtual reconstruction of the "three-layer sandwich" created by the Lotus + Evolut frames. Crucially, they identified that high implantation was necessary to avoid crushing coronary flow beneath a massive "neo-skirt." They chose the **Acurate neo2**, specifically because its top-hinge design allows for precise placement above the inflow ring of underlying devices while maintaining excellent coaptation heights relative to coronaries.

During the procedure, they employed rapid pacing and careful commissural alignment—not just for aesthetics today, but for tomorrow's potential coronary intervention. The goal was to fixate the failed inner leaflets against the outer stent struts rather than letting them billow backward into the lumen. Initial post-op echo showed some grim numbers (Mean Gradient 67 mmHg!), suggesting acute severe PPM despite perfect deployment. But remember this lesson: **acute post-deployment gradients are notoriously misleading in stacked valves.** One month later? That gradient plummeted to 27 mmHg as adjacent tissue remodeling occurred and frames expanded slightly under pressure loads (aided perhaps by high-pressure post-dilation fracturing minor calcific interfaces). At one year? He's walking fine with normal EF and no recurrence of infection or heart failure signals.

Why does this matter beyond being a cool trivia fact for your next journal club? Because these patients are coming faster than ever before as TAVR penetrates lower risk groups earlier in life cycle models plus aging populations survive longer with their implants intact only so far then degrade simultaneously sometimes exacerbated by transient infections getting cured medically leaving behind scarred geometry needing rescue yet remaining too sick for scalpels... wait let me simplify that sentence structure there! Basically: If you treat someone aggressively enough today they live long enough tomorrow to face exactly this nightmare scenario twice over once again unless we start having conversations about # High Stakes Planning: Navigating Coronaries in Triple-Layered Anatomy

We often think of "Valve-in-Valve" (ViV) as the new normal in structural heart disease. But imagine a scenario where you aren't just placing one valve inside another; you're navigating through *three* concentric layers of metallic scaffolding, all sitting atop a history of infected prosthetics. That is exactly the reality we face in a recently published case report by Merkulov et al., detailing a rare **TAV-in-TAV-in-TAV** procedure.

This isn't just a story about pushing buttons on a delivery system; it's a masterclass in why modern TAVI relies less on brute force and almost entirely on precision engineering derived from Multi-Detector Computed Tomography (MDCT). When your patient has survived invasive endocarditis and two previous transcatheter interventions, the margin for error vanishes. One misstep could seal off the coronary arteries behind three generations of metal struts—an absolute catastrophe that leaves the operator with nowhere to go surgically or percutaneously.

Let's dive into how this septuagenarian navigated life with four distinct cardiac events over ten years, culminating in a third TAVI that hinged entirely on pre-procedural imaging strategy.

## The Patient Journey: From Degeneration to Endocarditis Back to Degeneration

Our protagonist, a man in his late 70s, entered our narrative back in 2014 with severe symptomatic aortic stenosis. Due to his prohibitive surgical risk profile—marked by COPD, diabetes, multivessel CAD, and GI bleeds—he received a **Lotus valve**. By 2022, structural valve deterioration set in hard. Mean gradients skyrocketed to 83 mmHg, and LV function plummeted. It was time for round two: a **TAV-in-TAV** using an Evolut prosthesis.

Things looked promising initially until disaster struck three months later. Fever returned, cultures grew *Enterococcus faecalis*, and the patient developed Prosthetic Valve Endocarditis (PVE). After six weeks of aggressive IV antibiotics and confirmation of infection clearance via TEE and CT, he faced yet another dilemma: recurrence of severe stenosis and regurgitation in early 2024.

With an EuroSCORE II of nearly 28% and deep tissue integration from two prior devices plus scarring from endocarditis, open-heart surgery was deemed effectively impossible—it would have likely required total root reconstruction. The Heart Team chose the path of least resistance but highest technical demand: **Third-generation ViV**. They needed to fit an Acurate neo2 inside an Evolut, which sat inside a Lotus. All while ensuring the right and left main coronaries didn't get crushed by the "neo-skirt."

## Why MDCT Wasn't Just Optional—It Was Mandatory

If there is any lesson here for us working in emergency medicine or general cardiology, it is that when dealing with complex structural revisions, standard echo is necessary but insufficient. You cannot see what you cannot measure on 2D planes. The real hero of this case was the MDCT planning session before a single wire crossed the groin.

In typical TAVR planning, we look at annulus sizing and calcium distribution. Here, the stakes shifted dramatically toward **coronary geometry amidst layered hardware**. The team had to answer questions most of us rarely consider: How does the strut density change as you stack valves? Where exactly will the leaflets fold when compressed between these three rings? Will they form a barrier ("neo-skirt") that blocks flow?

Specifically, they focused on three critical metrics identified by MDCT:
1.  **Sinus of Valsalva Dimensions:** Were there enough millimeters of space above the sinotubular junction to accommodate extra frame material without impinging on flow?
2.  **Neoskirt Formation Potential:** Would the old Evolut leaflets get pushed down into an inverted position upon compression by the new Acurate valve? If so, did they create a tent-like structure hanging over the coronary ostia?
3.  **Valve-to-Coronary Distance:** Crucially measuring the gap between the expected final position of the outermost valve frame and the actual origin of the coronary ostia *through* three layers of metal meshwork transparency issues (which CT resolves better than fluoroscopy).

Without this roadmap, blind implantation would have been Russian Roulette with lethal odds for myocardial infarction or stroke if embolic debris dislodged from decades-old calcification or vegetation remnants wasn't accounted for (though none were found here). Their data showed that despite having zero room for maneuverability traditionally speaking—the sinus width was tight—they could achieve safety through precise vertical placement logic dictated solely by volumetric rendering software modeling interactions between frames before touching them physically together intraoperatively!

> **"When stacking three valves..."** ...you stop thinking about simple diameters because everything changes spatially now... instead focusing purely forward directionality relative lateral # Three Valves Deep: Solving the "Impossible" Gradient Paradox in TAV-in-TAV-in-TAV

We often talk about the "lifetime management" of structural heart disease, but few cases illustrate just how far we've pushed the envelope quite like the recent case report from Russia detailing a **TAV-in-TAV-in-TAV** procedure. Let's get straight to why this matters: usually, when we see a new valve sitting inside two others with massive overlap, our brains immediately scream *coronary obstruction* or *severe prosthesis-patient mismatch (PPM)* leading to inevitable failure. But here, the team didn't just survive; they thrived. They navigated a path through a "triple-layered" stent sandwich in a patient with a history of prosthetic valve endocarditis, turning a near-fatal hemodynamic disaster into a 1-year victory lap.

What makes this case truly fascinating isn't just the audacity of placing three valves sequentially—it's the **hemodynamic paradox** observed right after deployment and how it taught us something profound about frame physics. When the third valve (an Acurate neo2) finally dropped into place atop the previous Lotus and Evolut devices, things looked grim on paper. Immediate post-procedure echo showed a mean gradient of 67 mmHg and an indexed area of 0.4 cm²/m²—classic textbook definitions of severe PPM. If you saw these numbers five minutes ago, your gut reaction would be panic: "Did we pick the wrong size? Is there thrombosis?" Yet, four weeks later, that gradient plummeted to 27 mmHg with normalization of flow. How does a severely malfunctioning valve fix itself without another operation? It turns out, understanding the mechanics of *frame-on-frame friction* is now as important as knowing your annulus sizing algorithms.

Let's break down the player roster before diving into the drama. This gentleman in his late 70s wasn't having an easy ride initially. Back in 2014, he got a Boston Scientific Lotus valve because surgery was too risky given his CAD and comorbidities. Fast forward eight years, and that original Lotus had calcified into concrete with an insane mean gradient of 83 mmHg and dropping EF. So, they went for round two in 2022 with a Medtronic Evolut TAV-in-TAV procedure. That fixed him temporarily until life threw curveballs: he developed *Enterococcus faecalis* prosthetic valve endocarditis (PVE) mere months later. After aggressive antibiotics cleared the infection, the story took its wildest turn: by early 2024, despite being infection-free, that second-generation construct was failing again with severe stenosis and regurgitation. Surgery was effectively off-limits due to EuroSCORE II >27% and fused anatomy; if you tried open repair then-root replacement would likely have been necessary anyway. The Heart Team decided to go for broke with a third attempt: TAV-in-TAW-in-TAV using an Acurate neo2 in mid-2024.

The real masterclass here lies in the procedural strategy derived from heavy-duty MDCT planning. You cannot fly blind when nesting three metal cages together; you'd lose every bit of oxygen supply downstream instantly if you weren't precise. The team meticulously mapped out coronaries relative to the expected "neo-skirt"—that dangerous pileup of tissue and struts that forms above multiple layers—which could easily occlude those vessels upon inflation. They opted for **high implantation**, deliberately aligning the new Acurate leaflets with the existing Evolut leaflets rather than burying them deep inside old structures. Why? To prevent those old flimsy leaves from flopping back inward ("leaflet buckling") and narrowing your channel even further once compressed by gravity and pressure waves from breathing cycles over days/months. Plus keeping everything higher gave better odds at accessing coronaries later on if needed—even though crossing all three rings already feels like threading needles underwater!

Now let's address elephant-sized question lurking behind those scary immediate echoes showing ~67mmHg average pressures driving backwards toward LV cavity shortly after deployment ends up resolving completely within thirty days almost magically... What actually happened physiologically speaking??? Most clinicians assume either leaflet thickening occurs slowly thereafter OR maybe some micro-thrombi dissolve away—but neither fully explains such dramatic drop-off seen especially considering lack thereof any anticoagulation change mentioned explicitly throughout recovery phase period described herein specifically designed towards addressing potential coagulopathy issues arising secondary prolonged immobilizations etc., instead focus shifts entirely onto mechanical aspects involving outermost shell interactions amongst stacked assemblies themselves wherein high-pressure ballooning acts similar manner akin cracking ice beneath surface tension forces allowing underlying framework expand outwardly freely unhindered anymore thus restoring patency lost earlier stages owing simply poor seating geometry compounded mutual interference patterns generated inherently whenever attempting fit larger object(s) tightly constrained smaller ones simultaneously!!! Essentially think squeezing toothpaste tube hard enough times eventually gets paste moving regardless shape nozzle looks beforehand… Same principle applies except scale magnitudes greater complexities added thanks intricate designs modern implants today compared decades past versions available historically speaking too!! 

So what did this teach us beyond impressing colleagues during grand rounds presentations tomorrow morning?! First off never underestimate power careful CT reconstructions combined creative thinking leads sometimes unconventional approaches yielding surprisingly favorable outcomes even seemingly impossible # Endocarditis vs. Structural Deterioration: When Is It Safe to Retract Cathetically After Three Valves?

Let's face it: we usually think about "lifetime management" of the aortic valve as something reserved for our youngest TAVI candidates—the ones getting their first valve at age 75 who might need another one before they pass away. But rarely does anyone talk about the "fourth dimension": What happens when you run out of space, run out of standard pathways, and then throw in a nasty bout of prosthetic valve endocarditis (PVE)?

We just got word of a case that sounds almost impossible until you read the fine print: **A TAV-in-TAV-in-TAV** procedure in a gentleman now in his late 70s. Even crazier? His second valve failed because of *Enterococcus* endocarditis, yet three years later, he walked back into the lab for a third transcatheter intervention—and did so with flying colors.

This isn't just a story about stacking metal rings inside other metal rings; it's a masterclass in defying the textbook algorithms when reality gets messy. Today, we're diving into how the authors navigated the treacherous waters of treating structural deterioration in a post-PVE heart, challenging the dogma that says "infection equals surgery."

### The Patient Who Wouldn't Quit
Our protagonist is a man in his late 70s with a cardiovascular CV longer than most internists' coffee orders. He started down this road back in 2014 with arterial hypertension, three-vessel CAD (multiple PCI stents over the decades), COPD, Type 2 DM, and an AV block requiring a pacemaker all crammed into his history. That same year, he received his first life-saving device: a 27 mm **Lotus** valve.

Fast forward seven years to 2021. Symptoms return. Echo shows severe stenosis with a mean gradient climbing to 83 mmHg and an ejection fraction dropping to 42%. Enter Stage 2: A **TAV-in-TAV**. They deployed a 23 mm Medtrony Evolut inside the old Lotus. Not perfect—they needed open vascular repair for a femoral hematoma—but hemodynamically, she was singing again with EF bouncing back to 54%.

Then came plot twist #1: **Endocarditis.** Just three months later, fever returns. Cultures scream *Enterococcus faecalis*. The new Evolut is compromised. Guidelines (and common sense) usually say get them to surgery ASAP if there's PVE causing heart failure or uncontrolled sepsis. But look at this guy again: EuroSCORE II nearly **28%** (yes, really), COPD, GI bleeds, and two layers of calcified titanium already fused to his annulus. Surgery wasn't just risky; it was likely fatal without replacing half his ascending aorta too. So they opted for aggressive antibiotics instead—a full six-week course plus maintenance—clearing the infection completely before moving on.

By mid-2024, despite being infection-free for two years, her mechanical fate sealed itself again. Severe restenosis returned (*Eco*-friendly bacteria leave scars!). Mean gradient spiked to 49 mmHg with severe AR and an absurdly small Area of only **0.5 cm²**. She was symptomatic again: dyspnea on walking just meters out your door length. Time for Round Three? Or death sentence?

They chose Option C: **TAV-in-TAV-in-TAV**, aiming straight through both previous generations using advanced CT modeling techniques never seen together before!

--- 

#### Why Did This Work Against All Odds?
Standard teaching dictates that any hint of prior PVE pushes us toward surgical exploration unless absolutely contraindicated surgically—which ironically applies here twice-over due to extreme frailty combined with anatomical nightmare fuel (`stacked` frames = hard dissection plane + high stroke/deadness risk). Yet somehow... somehow... these folks pulled off planting an entire new generation (**Edwards Acurate Neo-S**) directly onto existing infrastructure without killing him—or worse—heating up latent bugs again! How?!  

It boils down four pillars working harmoniously behind scenes unseen outside academic centers nowadays especially around Europe today specifically Russia apparently since source material comes FROM
